# Atlas's map engine

Atlas draws its map with its own engine. This is the one document about it: what it is, how it works, what exists and what is left. It replaces every earlier plan, brief and note about the engine. Those are archived in `cache/claude-session/archive/engine-2026-10/` as history, and nothing here needs them.

## What it is

- **The centerpiece of the app, purpose built for Atlas.** It is done first, and the rest of the app builds on it.
- **Built from the ground up.** A C++23 core on Vulkan (`app/android/mapcore/cpp`), behind a small Kotlin façade (`app.atlas.mapcore.MapCore`). It draws vector tiles every frame the camera moves, at the screen's own density, and nothing at rest. A label stays where it is on the map and grows and shrinks with the zoom, like the map itself, and detail comes and goes with the zoom.
- **Global, and it builds itself.** The map covers the entire world, from the globe down to a single street. The phone gathers up-to-date map data for any place, at any time, and builds the map itself. Pre-built packs are additions, not a requirement.
- **Every detail OSM has, as smooth and efficient as other map apps, or more** (the user, 25 October). Nothing OSM maps is dropped from the data; each detail draws from the zoom where it can be seen, and goes on in the renderer's spirit where the renderer stops. A frame's cost follows what is on screen, not how much is mapped.
- **Independent.** The app never relies on a single source. Every input has more than one way in, and the map keeps working offline with what it has.
- **Matches the renderer.** The Python renderer (`render/*.py`, ported byte for byte to Kotlin in `app/android/render`) is the starting point for the look: its features, rules, colours and light.
  - The engine matches it exactly, except where the renderer is wrong.
  - The renderer is unfinished and has its issues. Each issue the engine fixes is named, such as melting buildings.
  - The renderer is frozen as the yardstick: the look is refined in the engine, which goes on in the renderer's spirit where it stops.
  - When the engine is finished, the renderer and its port are retired.
- **Custom where it counts.** The data model, tile format, rules, look, passes and shaders are Atlas's own. No general map engine and no third-party tile schema: OpenMapTiles is abolished.
  - An outside piece comes in only where it is cutting edge, fits, and plugs in without a sacrifice. Today that means Vulkan-Hpp, VMA, zstd, HarfBuzz, earcut.hpp and doctest in the core, Slang for the shaders, and Planetiler as the build tool for the world's mid zooms.
  - Each is pinned (`cpp/third_party/VERSIONS.md`) and can be replaced.
- **As cutting edge, optimized and efficient as possible, without sacrifices.** 60 fps while moving, idle and cool at rest, on any Vulkan phone. Every choice is made at run time from each phone's own facts, never tuned to one phone.
- **Easy to keep cutting edge.** The engine is built to be improved and refined for years: new hardware features, better techniques and newer libraries go in one piece at a time.

## The design

1. **The phone builds the map itself, anywhere, anytime, from more than one source.** It gathers up-to-date data for any place in the world, and the core builds the tiles on its workers:
   - **OpenStreetMap,** tile by tile with margins, so a building on a tile's edge is built once. The same data can come three ways, and the phone takes whichever answers:
     - several Overpass servers (Overpass is the renderer's own source), failing over from one to the next;
     - Geofabrik's regional extracts, read on the phone itself;
     - Atlas's own packs.
   - **The renderer's regional sources,** such as AHN and the tides in the Netherlands.
   - **Elevation** from the best model for each region (national lidar where it exists) and a global model elsewhere, each from more than one host.
   - Everything built is kept, so a place seen once works offline.

   **Packs are additions, not a requirement.**
   - Atlas's packer builds them on GitHub with the same code, for offline use and an instant first look. A packed tile and a tile the phone builds from the same data are the same, byte for byte.
   - They come from Geofabrik's extracts, which are made for bulk processing. Overpass suits a phone's live queries, but its public servers limit bulk use (about 10,000 queries and 1 GB a day).

   **The far zooms come from two small world bases,** built the same way:
   - a mid-zoom base, with Planetiler running Atlas's own profile into Atlas's format, published in more than one place;
   - a globe base, bundled in the APK.

   No live source can hand a phone whole regions and countries at once, so these are the only prebuilt data the map needs.
2. **Render tiles** (pack format 3) hold every value the look derives from the data, already derived: looks, traditions, heights, roof shapes, water levels, label candidates.
   - The core's stage P derives them on the phone; the packer runs the same code.
   - The values are stored in columns compressed with zstd, with a keep code per vertex for every simplification level.
   - A tile is memory-mapped, decoded straight into the GPU's heaps, and kept on disk once built. It is refreshed by asking Overpass only for what changed since.
3. **The style is the renderer's:** building traditions by country, and place-specific profiles wherever a place needs one.
   - Close up across Amsterdam: the living map's profile.
   - Williamsburg: `real.py`'s profile, if the user says yes.
   - Everywhere else: the regions' rules, with each country's building tradition.
   - At the planet: the Globe board's look.
   - The colours, widths, rules, traditions and profiles live in the engine's own style file, which the tiles carry. Refining the look, or adding a tradition or a profile, is an edit to data.
4. **Compact records, expanded on the GPU.** Walls, wall cells, windows, roof faces, trees and street furniture are records of a few bytes that the vertex stage expands, and repeated detail is instanced. Draws are indexed and batched per pipeline. meshoptimizer is the candidate for vertex-cache order and quantised vertices, if it measures better.
5. **One pass in tile memory.** The main render pass has a scene subpass and a composite subpass at 4x MSAA, with its attachments kept in tile memory where the GPU allows.
   - The composite applies the screen effects once per pixel: haze, vignette, grain, `mat`, the wash, cloud shadows and rain.
   - Offscreen passes run before it only where an effect needs one: blurs, crowns, reflections, glows.
   - Newer Vulkan features are used where the phone has them, picked at run time from VK facts: dynamic rendering with local reads, synchronization2, present timing, and fragment shading rate for the thermal ladder.
6. **True depth for everything that stands up.** Buildings, parts, roofs, trees and things are drawn with a real depth buffer, kept in tile memory beside the samples, and tile by tile from the nearest for early rejection. They occlude correctly at every angle.
   - The renderer orders whole items by one point each, so at some angles its buildings melt into each other. The engine does not copy that.
   - The depth is reversed: near / k.z, in float with stencil where the phone has it (D32S8), else 24-bit, with the depth tests GREATER (`shaders/shared/painter.slang`).
   - The ground keeps the renderer's layering on its one plane: land cover, areas, roads, water and shadows, in its order. Its painter slots take the far end of the depth range, behind every true depth, so what stands up is drawn over the ground as the renderer draws it. Terrain (step 6) gives the ground a surface's true depth.
   - Where faces of one building touch, two small leads keep the renderer's order: a later cell's edge covers an earlier one's, and a roof covers the edge of its walls. Faces of two buildings that touch meet where their geometry puts them.
   - Flat detail on a wall (cells, windows, sashes, shutters, plinths, cornices, shop glazing, doors, numbers) keeps the renderer's exact shapes. It is expanded on the GPU from the wall's record and drawn clipped to its own wall, so nothing fights for depth.
   - The phone draws true depth alone since 30 October 2026. The user: "Why do we have these different looks? Its overcomplicated. There should be one unified and nothing else", then "The painter order setting can go unless you think otherwise". The renderer's painter's order stays in the core for its own checks only: a switch (`FrameOptions::painter`) draws it, the owner maps are drawn under it, and the vk tests hold the buildings, doors, items and trees to the renderer's order with it. Nothing on the phone turns it on. Features are built and tested for true depth, and the painter's order is kept working only as far as those checks use it.
   - The painter ranks stay, for what is translucent and for the detail bands. So the twin still emits in the renderer's order, and only its test raster gains depth.
   - What stands out from a wall (chimneys, dormers, hoist beams, shopfronts, signs, terraces) is geometry with depth.
7. **Light and sun.** The renderer's light model and themes for the moment, and the sun test at every sample point, recomputed on the workers when the sun moves 0.5°. The moment's weather comes from more than one source, and the sun alone sets the moment when none answers.
8. **Text.**
   - Names on the ground (water and canal names along their ways) are shaped by HarfBuzz as Android shapes them, and drawn from the glyphs' outlines (Slug).
   - Place names in every script are sprites drawn by Android's own text stack.
   - The core places them, with collisions and fades. Once placed, every label stays fixed where it is on the map and grows and shrinks with the zoom, like the map (the user, 21 October: "The text should be fixed where it is and only update in size"; then "every label stays where it is and grows and shrinks with the zoom, like the map"): no re-placing at rest, no sliding or jumping between its spots while the map moves, no fixed screen size. Since labels and the map scale together, a placement free of collisions stays free at every zoom.
     - **A zoom band per label.** Each label scales with the map only within a band around the zoom it is drawn for, about 0.7 to 1.5 times its size there; beyond it, it fades out and the next level's labels fade in (quarters and districts out of town, streets and buildings close in).
     - **A readable floor.** No label draws below a minimum size, which follows Android's text-size setting.
     - Names over the map, of places and of waters alike (the quarters, the IJ, the Amstel), stay level on the screen when the map turns: they only stay put and grow and shrink with it (the user, 21 October). Names on the ground, such as the canal names, follow their water and read upright, with hysteresis.
9. **Levels** run from the street to the planet: full detail, a simplified level from the keep codes, blocks of merged footprints, the mid-zoom base, the globe. Fades and a triangle governor mean nothing pops and nothing is a hole.
10. **Work only when needed.** Frames come on vsync only while something changes, with ADPF hints. Tiles, the sun and the noise fields are done on background workers. Nothing is done per frame that can be done once.
11. **Deterministic everywhere, exact wherever the result depends on it.**
    - The core's maths library (`pymath`: fdlibm as Java defines it, with Python's rounding and random stream) gives the same bits on every phone and host. So frames can be compared, and no decision differs between devices.
    - Where the renderer's arithmetic decides a pixel, the engine uses it:
      - the noise fields byte for byte, on the CPU, and on the GPU with exact fused multiply-adds where the device passes a byte check;
      - the haze and vignette in whole numbers.
    - Plain GPU float is used only where it gives the same pixels.
    - Neither the look nor the speed is given up for the other.
12. **Built to keep improving:**
    - each pass, its shaders, its geometry builder and its twin live in files of their own;
    - device features are picked at run time, so a new extension is one more branch, not a rewrite;
    - outside pieces are updated in commits of their own, with the checks' numbers before and after;
    - a desktop render tool draws any view and moment to a PNG on the Mac, to judge the look without a phone and to make the boards;
    - the twin, the engine's own references and the checks make every refactor safe.

**What the rethink of 20 October changed** from the old plan:
- **The renderer is a yardstick, not the eternal reference.** It is frozen, the look is refined in the engine, and the engine fixes the renderer's issues and replaces it.
- **Buildings never melt.** True depth for what stands up, instead of the renderer's painter's order, which the old plan copied on purpose.
- **The bar is the renderer's result exactly, apart from its named issues, at full speed.** Exactness is built wherever the result depends on it. The heavier fallbacks (integer blending, analytic coverage) are built only if a check shows a difference.
- **The phone builds the map itself, anywhere, from live data.** The look's derivations move into the core as C++, so the phone builds natively, and the packer uses the same code. Packs become optional additions, for offline use and a fast first look.
- **All data is Atlas's own.** OpenMapTiles is gone; Planetiler stays only as a build tool, running Atlas's own profile.
- **The checks that prove the result stay; the rest go.** Every check already built keeps running, and the full set of the renders' views runs nightly. The old plan's extra gates that were never built are not built.
- **No cut changes the result.** A simplification may change how the engine gets there, never what Amsterdam or any rendered place looks like. The engine's references prove it.
- **Two pieces get simpler.** VK check (never built) becomes VK facts' hash lines. Taps on buildings use a hit test against the tile's own buildings instead of an ID pass.
- **One agent works through one ordered list,** instead of waves of packages. The engine comes before the rest of the app.

## Checks

**While the renderer exists, it is the yardstick:**
1. **Parity with the port:**
   - the core's stage P against the port's loader, record by record and field by field, from the same data;
   - the sun's answers, stage T's points, tile decoding, and pymath bit for bit;
   - the phone's tiles against the packer's, byte for byte.
2. **Element by element:** the twin (the core's per-frame code and the shaders' maths compiled as C++) emits what the GPU will draw. `L1Test` matches it against the port's SVG at the boards' views and the sweeps: in order for the ground, item by item for what stands up. Every new pass brings its twin and its cases. On the Mac it also matches the port's and Schiphol's close views by day and at night and their overviews by day (their element packs are the Mac's alone; CI has the canal ring's), both sides without the regions' height survey, from render packs the core's stage P cuts in the test.
3. **The GPU against the twin** on lavapipe, per pass.
4. **Likeness:** the smoke cases (the Westerkerk home and the Main board, by day and at night) against the port's renders on every push.
5. **The full set:** every named view of the renders, the sweeps and the sizes, nightly on CI. The planted failures that already exist keep running.
   - The nightly's full tier runs in two shards. Each judges the planted failures whose base case falls in it, and the smoke tier judges every kind on every push. The nightlies first ran on 23 October, once `app` became the default branch: the second shard holds only the board's plants (shift, drops), and `PlantedTest` failed it for want of the paint plant until its reference set said it was a shard.

**Where the renderer is wrong, the engine does it right.** The difference is named in the case files (melting buildings is the first), so the yardstick never pulls the engine back to the renderer's issues.

**The engine's own references, from step 1.** These are its frames at fixed views and moments: the boards' views first, then places around the world from step 4. They are stored per engine version, and every finished part is held to them. Every simplification is proven against them: Amsterdam and every other rendered place must turn out the same. Each version holds the frames of the Mac's lavapipe, recorded with the version. From version 26 it also holds those of CI's lavapipe-x86_64, so CI holds the frames on Linux too. CI's first run of a new version leaves them in its likeness report (`engine/engine.json`), to paste in.

**Always:**
6. **CI** runs every check on every push to `app`, side by side on GitHub's runners (the user, 30 October: "Optimize the flow. All the tests and suites we constantly run and wait on take too much time and slow us down"). A push to `app` never cancels another push's run: each commit gets its word, and the nightly alpha chooses among them. Anywhere else a newer push or run by hand cancels the older run of its branch.
   - `core.yml`: host builds and tests on x86_64 and arm64 under the validation layer (ASan and UBSan, TSan, the release flags), fuzzing, clang-tidy with both builds' databases, clang-format, the canaries, the NDK builds and the rule table.
     - The core's tests are one ctest entry per test file: `tests/<area>/<name>.cpp` is `<area>/<name>`, labelled with its area, and `vk` for a `_vk_test.cpp`. `atlas_tests_rest` runs any case no file's entry takes, and `atlas_tests_split` proves that each case runs exactly once. A host job runs the entries as many at a time as the runner has cores, each test process with one lavapipe thread; an entry whose checks time the runner runs alone and first, with lavapipe's every thread (`gpu/standin_vk_test`, whose steady frame the release build holds to half a second).
     - A host job whose tests would run long is split into shards (`host-debug x86_64 1/3`). `tools/ctest/shards.py` gives each shard its entries by the seconds `tools/ctest/costs.tsv` measured, the costliest first, so every shard computes the same split; host-debug x86_64's shards run their entries once more with every ASan check and no Vulkan, and that run's seconds (the column `host-debug-x86_64:novk`) count in each shard's wall too. `core-tests` then holds each job's shards to having run each entry exactly once, puts each shard's seconds and the entries' peak memory in the run's summary, and keeps the table measured again, each entry's seconds the mean of the table's and the run's, since the x86_64 runners' speeds vary from run to run (artifact `ctest-costs`). When the shards drift apart, or a new test file costs much, copy that `costs.tsv` over `tools/ctest/costs.tsv`; a job that needs another shard gets another line in the matrix. A file whose cases take too long for one entry runs as parts (`_test_parts` in `CMakeLists.txt`, `<area>/<name>:<k>`): each part takes the next cases of the file in their order, the last part the rest.
     - ccache keeps each job's compiled units: one cache entry a job, kept by a push to `app` and by the nightly, which delete the job's older entries; every other run only reads them. The canaries build host-debug's and host-tsan's trees with one planted test more, from those entries. The planted sin is `-DATLAS_PLANTED_PLATFORM_SIN=ON`, defined for `base/pymath/fdlibm/Trig.cpp` alone.
     - clang-tidy is two jobs, `tidy` with the host build's database and `tidy-android` with the Android build's, each in two shards that take half the units apiece (by a hash of the path). A unit is checked again only when its key changes (`tools/tidy/cached.py`: the unit preprocessed with its comments, its flags, `.clang-tidy` and the clang-tidy's version); the nightly checks every unit without the cache. clang-format checks every file in `tidy`'s first shard, whose clang-tidy runs only after it passes.
   - `android.yml`: the APKs (`apk`), the app's unit tests (`unit-app`), every other module's (`unit-rest`), `:mapcorecheck`'s JNI tests (`mapcorecheck`) and the likeness smoke tier (`likeness`) are jobs of their own. Once the APKs are built, `ci/smoke.sh` runs in five parts, each on an emulator of its own: `bench` (the home and the filters, then the bench APK's truth phase), `vk` (VK facts, VK bench and the crash guard, the pills, the map) and `screens-1` to `screens-3` (every third of the other screens), each keeping its artifact `atlas-emulator-<part>`. `emulator-coverage` holds the parts to each screen and phase once. The hash lines must read the host build's hashes as `ci/hashes.env` holds them (kept outside the workflows: the nightly alpha's token may not tag a commit whose workflow files differ from the default branch's). The nightly has a concurrency group of its own. An emulator that dies fails its part within about 5 minutes (smoke.sh's watchdog, "DEVICE GONE"): on 28 October 7f28e5d's emulator went offline at the walk screen, and every adb call after it waited until the job's limit, 110 minutes.
   - Outside downloads survive their failures (4 November: a system image that came broken, Robolectric's own fetch failing, Maven Central's 429 Too Many Requests, the emulator's device offline for a few seconds). The emulator's SDK packages (the system image, the newest stable emulator, the pinned build) come as the archives the SDK repository lists, each checked against its SHA-1 and fetched again whole while it does not check out (`ci/sdkpkg.py`), kept in the caches by package and revision and installed where sdkmanager finds them, so the emulator action's sdkmanager fetches nothing; the pinned build replaces the newest just before the emulator starts, and the lavapipe jobs take it the same way. Gradle tries a fetch 8 times over about 4 minutes (`gradle.properties`); Robolectric's Android is a dependency Gradle fetches and hands to Robolectric to use offline (the root `build.gradle.kts`); `apk` runs `testDependencies`, so the Gradle entry it keeps holds every module's test artifacts, and the test jobs (the nightly's likeness-full and vkcheck-asset too) restore apk's entries and say what Gradle still fetched (a notice). smoke.sh runs an adb command the device's absence refused again once the device is back (`adb reconnect offline`, then up to 120 s), with a warning, and takes a log a check reads again when it came cut short; one not had whole fails the part ("DUMP FAILED").
   - A run by hand on a branch (`gh workflow run core.yml --ref <branch>`, the same for `android.yml`) runs everything, reading `app`'s caches. To measure a change to CI warm, `-f keep_caches=true` keeps the branch's own caches; delete them after (`gh cache list --ref refs/heads/<branch>`).
7. **The user's phone at each alpha:**
   - VK facts;
   - VK bench, with presented frames at p50 ≤ 16.7 ms and p95 ≤ 20 ms on the paths;
   - the ten-minute soak, with no climb in thermal headroom;
   - a look.

## What exists so far

Nothing is finished. Each part below is a first version on `app`. It gets reworked wherever the design above asks for more.

| Part | State |
|---|---|
| Toolchain and CI | NDK r30, CMake presets for the hosts and Android, Slang shaders checked at build time, lavapipe under the validation layer |
| Vulkan device layer | VK facts, the crash guard, frame configurations, both frame routes, the pipeline cache and the SurfaceView host; the VK facts and VK bench screens, measured on the user's phone; VK facts' hash lines (pymath, the sun, the noise fields, the ground, the twin), held to the host build's on CI's emulator, and its composite check, behind a guard stage of its own |
| Data | render tiles, the packer's `render-tiles` and the core's reader (fuzzed); the core's writer of tiles and packs, with their head and style, the packer's bytes; the core's reader of Overpass's JSON, the living map's and a region's load from it with every look (the port's records) and their cut into tiles (the packer's bytes); windows, one L14 tile built from its box's data as a pack of its own, built again after a week from Overpass's changes merged into the data it keeps (and from that data alone when the core's version changes), a frame cell's windows one pack in the store, drawn alone or beside the bundled packs; the canal ring bundled, which draws the Westerkerk's place too (the Westerkerk's own pack stays in the APK for VK facts' hash lines); render packs that build for `amsterdam` and `grachten` |
| Numerics and style | pymath; the light, the theme and `ScreenLayers`; the style file (`app/android/mapcore/style/engine.jsonc`), which every render pack carries and the core reads, typed, as a pack opens; the rule table, which lists every line of the renderer that draws |
| Frame core | camera, ladder, cover, residency, heaps (one buffer and one virtual block of a budget as large as the device binds, `scene::heap_budget`: 64 to 256 MB, 128 MB on every PowerVR; a tile that finds no room waits for a range to go), ranks and pass registry; the composite (haze, vignette, `mat`'s rule, grain, cloud shadows, rain, golden wash); the twin and L1; the coarser pack around the active one, chosen as the smallest pack that holds it; the packs' own stage T (`Pass::build_pack`) |
| Sun | the sun test on the workers, equal to the port on every sample of 21 board renders; a lane for the pack drawn and one for the coarser pack around it |
| Ground and water | drawn and registered; L1 matched in all 11 cases; the soft shadows on the port's own filter grid, their boxes and fade bit for bit |
| Buildings, first half | walls, parts, every roof shape and houseboats, with sun bits, edges and the roofs' thin edges, at their true depth (a pair the renderer melts is drawn right; the core's painter switch, for its checks, draws the renderer's order); their windows, dark or lit, with the glass gradients, and close up deep, with their reveals, sills and sash bars; their plinths, cornices and shop glazing; held to the port's polygons and on lavapipe to a depth raster of the twin with its strokes; registered |
| Trees and things | every mapped tree with its trunk, limbs and clumps in their tones as the renderer draws it with weather (its generic tree without), the street lamps, benches, racks, baskets and bollards and the bridges' railings, each on one plane at its true depth; the sett roads' courses on the ground, each road's lines one layer; a region's towers, turbines, cranes, masts, gantries, conveyors, coaster tracks, planted woods and power lines at their true depth; held to the port by L1, the region items at the port, Schiphol, the Efteling and ARTIS too |
| Text | shaping, glyphs, canal names (read from the whole pack), and the board's labels drawn by the core, fixed on the map and scaled with it; registered, the fonts from the map's resources |
| Noise fields, thin strokes | WebKit's turbulence byte for byte (scalar and NEON); `ObjectStrokes` |
| Frosted glass | drawn by the core under the chrome |
| Likeness harness | references from the port, candidates from the host build, with their owner maps (real since 21 October); the engine's own references (`likeness/engine.json`); the desktop render tool |
| The app | the core draws the maps where it has tiles, under today's map and the overlays, cross-fading to today's map (the port on Canvas) elsewhere; the honest state where the phone cannot draw (three device losses included) |

The local branches are history; `wip/build-pack`, `wip/buildings-registration`, `wip/app-map` and `wip/text-registration` landed on 20 October 2026, and `wip/exact-haze`'s last commit (the rest was on `app` already) the same night. The old builders' worktrees went to the Trash on 20 October 2026; their work is on `app` or these branches.

## What the deep dive found (20 October)

Before anything is built on them, the app's skeleton, the core and the modules were read for what should be done differently:
- **The core's painter's order is one contained piece.** It lives in `shaders/shared/painter.slang` and `frame/Ranks`. True depth changes what the objects' vertex stage writes, the ground's hand-over and the depth format, not the frame core.
- **The tile store already opens packs from memory, files and remote ranges.** So tiles built on the phone become local packs, with no second tile path (step 3).
- **The app's map still pulls in the CPU stack.** `:mapview` exports `:render` and `:anchor` to the app; they leave with the CPU map at the end of step 3 (the port itself in step 7).
- **The rest holds:** the device layer, frame core, scene, tile reader, sun, text, noise fields, the façade and the toolchain are current and fit the design.

The app's own findings were app work, done outside this plan on 20 October:
- one persistent map at the app's root instead of a map per screen (`MapStage`);
- one set of Overpass servers: the map's builds ask the app's own, in its order;
- a second weather source (MET Norway);
- the app's features off OpenMapTiles. Places, addresses, street names and bridges read the map's own packs through `LocalData`, and Overpass where no pack reaches: one request per route for its steps' names, one per route for its bridges. OpenFreeMap, the tile cache, the tile readers and the Planetiler step are gone. Packs built on the phone are built again after a week. An offline region's pack is read by the map and the features, and a region beyond the published packs has its pack built on the phone (up to about 1,000 km²).

The engine builds on them: step 3's "the core asks, the app fetches" has its fetching side in `LocalData` and the pack library.

## Where the time goes, and the gains (24 October)

Read from the code, from what the core counts on the Mac, and from the device survey of 18 October (`cache/claude-session/archive/engine-2026-10/briefs/device-survey.md`, its data in `/Volumes/Download/atlas/survey`: the core's rules over gpuinfo's 8,613 Android reports, 45 GPU families read report by report). The engine's own map has no phone numbers yet: VK bench's map paths have run only on CI's emulator, whose frames carry no timings, and the user's phone has measured only the stand-in (18 October: 677k triangles in 8 to 9 ms of GPU on Mali, in the low teens on the PowerVR, whose surface gestures still dropped 145 to 182 frames each). By its driver (25.3@6908880) the survey puts the user's phone among the Pixel 10s and 11s (PowerVR D- or C-Series, DXT or CXTP-48-1536). Each gain keeps the frames as they are, held by the checks it names; in each group the largest comes first. The one problem already measured on the user's phone, the gestures' dropped frames, is 11.

**The frame**

1. **The buildings draw only what they draw.**
   - Every primitive of every visible cell goes to the GPU: a wall or a near cell as the 16 points of its dilated outline (14 triangles), a roof's face or an n-gon's triangle as 5 points, and each plain primitive again as its strokes' 30 vertices. Each vertex decides its whole primitive again (`building_shape`: up to eight camera transforms, the near plane's cut, the box against the frame, the area floor, the snap, then the outline's corners and arcs), so a wall is decided 16 times a frame. A primitive that draws nothing (another detail band, facing away, outside the frame, under the area floor, beyond the radius cut) still runs all its vertices.
   - `buildings_vk_test` submits 3.21M triangles in 81 draws at the Main board for 26,587 elements drawn and 50,082 stroke segments, and 932k at the home for 4,323 elements and 5,553 segments. Its frames are engine mode, whose radius cut leaves more of what is submitted undrawn than the app's map does, but the app's map submits the same. `FrameStatsTest`'s 2.06M and 2.66M buildings' triangles against §3's 600k are these.
   - a. Done (24 October): **the near cells only where an item is near.** A near cell draws only for an item nearer than 460 m (its rank's near bit), and the densest tile's mix (60,672 walls, 58,097 near cells, 62,360 roof faces and n-gon triangles) puts 44 % of its buildings' triangles in the near cells. Stage F leaves out a tile's near cells unless one of its buildings is that near (the ranks' last rows, far to near), and then a cell's unless one of the buildings with near cells in it is (`BuildingTile::near_items`, from stage T). The engine's references are unchanged (version 7). `buildings_vk_test` submits 2.33M triangles at the Main board (3.21M before) and 797k at the home (932k), and at the Main board no near cell is drawn at all. The roofs' strokes (1.15M of the board's 2.33M) are now its largest kind: c.
   - b. Done (24 October; deciding once, 3 November): **drawn compacted** (`passes/Kept.cpp`). Before the main pass three compute stages run over the frame's jobs (every run of the walls', the near cells' and the roofs' lists, in their draws' order, at most 64 primitives a job):
     - the cull tests each primitive as `building_shape` first tests it: its rank, its detail variant, the weather, and its facing with a margin (`building_may_draw`, which leaves out only what `building_shape` would draw nothing of, whatever either stage's float arithmetic does);
     - the scan gives each job's candidates their places in their list, so no atomics choose the order, and where two faces tie in depth the same one still wins;
     - the scatter writes them there, and decides each of a list's first kept walls and near cells once, into its shape record (below).

     Each list is then drawn in chunks of its static index buffer's primitives, through indirect draws the scan writes, a call each (`multiDrawIndirect` is false on the survey's Mali G52 to G78 rows). A chunk of records places each point from its primitive's record (`walls_shape_vs`), and the vertex stage decides the rest at each point as before (`walls_kept_vs`, `roofs_kept_vs`). It stays within the survey's floor: the scan in shared memory (subgroups are one lane on PowerVR Rogue), workgroups of 64, seven more storage buffer bindings (the compute stages see 18, the vertex stage 16, of the floor's 35). Where the device cannot make the kept pipelines, the lists are drawn as their runs; where it cannot make the records' three (their scatter, their draw and its twin while a tile fades), every kept primitive is decided at each point.
     - The engine's references are unchanged (version 7), and so are `buildings_vk_test`'s raster and L1.
     - The buildings' triangles the GPU draws (`FrameStatsTest`, reading back what the cull kept): 258k at the Main board of the 1.18M submitted (2.06M before a), 151k at the canal ring's first sweep camera of 1.48M (2.66M), 50k at the home of 356k. These frames are engine mode, whose radius cut leaves more undrawn than the app's map does.
     - **Deciding once** (3 November). The records' scatter (`kept_shapes_cs`) decides each of a list's first kept walls and near cells for the frame as `walls_vs` decides it, into its shape record of 80 bytes (`BuildingShapeRecord` in `building.slangh`: a word of its corners' count, whether the app asks about its building, its lead and its painter slot; its five corners; its plane and the span its reciprocal depths are held to; its colour), and the vertex stage places each of its 16 points from it (`walls_shape_vs`: the outline's point, its plane's reciprocal depth there, its depth). A shape that draws nothing writes a word of no corners, which puts its points nowhere. While a tile fades (the standing fades) the records' draw has its twin, `walls_shape_fading_vs`, which takes its tile's fade through the kept place its record stands for, as `walls_kept_vs` takes it through its own (`kept_shapes_cs` writes every candidate's job and lane, a record's too). In SPIR-V's arithmetic instructions a wall decided at each point runs 16 × 872 (`walls_kept_vs`), from its record 717 once (`kept_shapes_cs`) and 16 × 228 (`walls_shape_vs`; 16 × 254 while a tile fades): 9,600 fewer, 69 %, for its 80 bytes written and 80 read, 60 a byte.
       - A list's records are a whole number of its chunks (4,096 walls), or all its primitives: as many as its frame slot's last frame kept with a quarter more, the walls' first, within the slot's budget, a sixteenth of the heaps' (`scene::heap_budget`, 64 to 256 MB) and at most 16 MiB (209,715 records). Beyond the budget, and in a slot's first frame, the chunks are decided at each point.
       - The roofs have none. A roof's record would save 5 × 859 − (767 + 5 × 65), about 3,200 instructions, for the same 160 bytes: 20 a byte, about what a tiler spends moving them (the user's PowerVR DXT-48-1536 runs about 0.85 T instructions a second against about 55 GB/s, 15 to 20 a byte), where a wall's 60 are three to four times that.
       - What they cost. A frame slot's records grow by half again what its frame asks, within the budget, and keep that room. At the Westerkerk pitched 48° at a bearing of 22° (`pinch_audit_vk_test` at 360 × 800 px at 0.875, from the Netherlands' windows and Noord-Holland's lean levels), at 5, 7, 9 and 10 km the walls kept are 67,495, 117,098, 140,775 and 144,407 (of 237,629, 387,203, 390,991 and 385,589), every one from its record: 5.15, 8.93, 10.74 and 11.02 MiB written and read a frame, both slots' buffers 19.69 MiB at 5 km and 32 MiB from 7 km (the budget's 16 MiB a slot, on the user's phone as on lavapipe, whose heaps' budget is 256 MB; 4 MiB a slot on a device of 64 MB). By the counts above, at 10 km the walls decided at each point run 2.01 G instructions a frame and from records 0.63 G, for 23 MB moved: on the user's PowerVR at its peak about 2.4 ms against 0.74 ms and 0.4 ms, so the bytes cost about a quarter of the work they save.
       - Measured on lavapipe (relative only: the Mac's, two threads, shared with other sessions' builds and tests throughout), with `ATLAS_PINCH_SHAPES=cycle-alone`: at each of those rests the buildings pass alone, its frames without the walls and roofs, with each decided at each point and with every chunk from records, in turn on the same build, over seven cycles (the cycles' medians, records less each at each point). Decided at each point the walls and roofs take 783, 1,532, 1,843 and 1,752 ms of the main pass; from records it takes 68, 90, 47 and 30 ms less, within the cycles' spread of several hundred ms, and the compute stages before it 10, 20, 24 and 20 ms more. At 72 × 160 px for the same 411 × 914 dp (`ATLAS_PINCH_SIZE`: the same walls kept, a twenty-fifth of the pixels), the walls and roofs take no less, 812, 1,902, 2,261 and 2,654 ms, so lavapipe spends their time on each triangle (its setup, binning and the few samples it covers), not on pixels; from records 193, 76 and 125 ms less at 7, 9 and 10 km (3 to 10 %), the compute stages 12 to 24 ms more, and at 5 km the cycles ran from 1.5 s less to 2.3 s more under other sessions' builds. Where records take two thirds of the vertex stage's arithmetic away, that is a vertex stage of about 5 to 15 % of lavapipe's walls' time, whose setup and binning run on its CPU threads; on a tiler they are fixed function, beside the shader cores that run the vertex stage.
       - The first picture: the records' three pipelines (a configuration makes 119, 116 before) cost it little through an empty pipeline cache. On lavapipe, with Mesa's shader cache off, in three interleaved rounds (`registry_vk_test`), the base's 98 pipelines (70c22f3e) took 3.64 to 3.96 s on one thread and 1.38 to 1.86 s on eight; with the records' two then, 3.49 to 3.77 s and 1.49 to 1.64 s (medians 3.71 against 3.74 s, and 1.41 against 1.51 s); with the standing fades' pipelines too, the 119 take 4.45 to 5.48 s and 1.27 to 1.80 s. The pipeline cache keeps them for the next start.
       - The same pixels: `buildings_vk_test`'s 110 frames and its pack cases' 10 are byte for byte as before, and each list drawn with every chunk from records, with one and with none (`Buildings::most_shape_chunks`) draws the same, as it does with every tile fading on the dither's levels from a quarter to three quarters (`Buildings::fade_every`), which dithers its walls; `world_vk_test`'s standing fades, their walls from records, go from the stand-ins' picture to the L14's as before (13 of 17 frames dithered, none of a fading tile drawn whole, the last frame 200.65 from the stand-ins'); `shape_record_test` holds each point a record places to the point deciding the wall there places, bit for bit (32,000 random walls at rest and moving, on float and 24-bit depth, the painter switch on and off); the engine's references (version 30), L1 and `FrameStatsTest`'s counts are unchanged. Not checked on the phone.
       - On the phone (4 November): VK bench draws the paths that keep the most walls, the home's pinch out to 8 km and pitch70's turn, on once each is measured, with the records on and off in turn (`MapCore.shapeRecords`, `frame::MapFrame::set_shape_records`: off is `Buildings::most_shape_chunks(0)`, each kept wall decided at each point and no records written), in slices of half a second two by two, the last on (on, off, off, on, … over the full bench's 30 s and the quick one's 6, off then on in CI's 1.5), so that heat and throttling meet both alike. Each side has its own line, `vkbench map home pinch records on: …` and `… records off: …`, with its GPU's time before, in and after the main pass; frames begun while the records were being set are left out. Where the frames draw no records either way, a line says why (`… records: not compared (…)`). VK bench reads the thermal headroom only in its soak, so these lines carry none. CI's checks read none of them, and CI's bench compares at the home's pinch alone: each setting waits for the frame in flight, which takes about 2 s at the far views on the emulator, so a comparison adds 2 to 5 s there. Held by `map_frame_vk_test` (on lavapipe the same walls kept either way, none drawn from records off and theirs on, the switch taken by the next frame builder made) and `VkBenchTest`.
   - c. **The roofs' strokes a segment per stroked edge.** The same compute stage keeps each stroked edge in order, and the strokes' draw runs 6 vertices for each: 0.30M vertex invocations at the Main board instead of 1.72M.
   - d. **The windows** likewise: only the kept windows' triangles (their refinement in step 2).
   - e. Later, where a device has `VK_EXT_mesh_shader`, a mesh stage could decide and emit in one step, without the shapes' buffer, if its primitives keep the draw's order: a branch chosen at run time from VK facts. Of the survey's rows only the Adreno 840 and 850 and the Xclipse 960 list it, no Mali or PowerVR, so the compute path stays the one most phones take.
   - Held by `buildings_vk_test`'s raster and the engine's references (the same primitives in the same order) and by L1 (the twin does not change). `FrameStatsTest` counts what the GPU keeps (its indirect counts read back) beside what is submitted.
2. **The composite's integers once a frame, not once a pixel.** The exact haze converts each of its 17 stops (25 at eye level) to 1/4096ths and searches them at every pixel, then divides; the vignette divides by the frame's width and height and takes an integer square root at every pixel. A GPU has no integer divider, so each division is a sequence of instructions, over 2.6M pixels at S1. The stand-in's float haze and vignette added 0.25 ms on Mali; the user found the exact ones seemed to cost frame time (21 October).
   - The haze's opacity depends on the upright row alone: a table of the frame's rows (2,400 at S1), made on the CPU once a frame with the twin's own integers (`composite_haze_alpha12`), leaves one read a pixel.
   - The vignette's u depends on the column alone and v on the row: their squares in two tables, made when the frame's size changes, leave an addition and the square root a pixel; the corner's opacity stays per frame.
   - Exact by construction: the same integers. `composite_vk_test` and the engine's references hold it.
   - Done (26 October): the composite pass fills three tables in stage F (`passes/Composite.cpp`), each as long as the output's longer side, into the frame slot's slice of a buffer of its own (set 2 binding 2, at a dynamic offset): the haze's opacity by row (`composite_haze_alpha12`), and the vignette's squared distances by column and by row (`composite_vignette_square`, with a mark of its own beyond the radius). The shader keeps the square root and the corner's interpolation (`composite_vignette_from_squares`), and the twin takes both opacities from the same functions at each pixel (`composite_steps_fx` over `composite_steps_alphas`). The vignette's tables are made every frame too: a few thousand integer divisions on the CPU.
3. **The two subpasses merged where the render pass draws.** The render pass with two subpasses is the route of 63.9 % of the survey's recent phone reports that run: Adreno 6xx and 7xx, Mali on r32 to r44 drivers, PowerVR Rogue, Maleoon 910 and 920, and Xclipse, which is no tiler. The dynamic route, the user's phone's, keeps the frame in one scope. Where a tiler's driver does not merge the scene's subpass and the composite's, the 4x attachments (about 83 MB at S1) go to memory and back every frame, a cost no other gain here comes near. VK facts can print `VK_EXT_subpass_merge_feedback`'s answer where the driver has it (Mali, PowerVR, Adreno 840 and 850); Adreno 6xx and 7xx, the route's largest group, do not have it, and VK bench's composite against one subpass (A0's +0.25 ms on Mali) answers there. Where a driver does not merge and has `VK_EXT_shader_tile_image` (Mali r44 and later, PowerVR C- and D-Series, Maleoon 920 and later) or rasterization-order access (Adreno 8xx, Mali), the composite can read the tile within the scene's own pass instead.
4. **The ground's draws indexed** (a refinement in step 2). A fill's vertex stage pulls its index from the ground heap in a draw without an index buffer, so every triangle runs its three vertices and no shaded vertex is reused. Indexed through the heap, a fill's vertices run about once each (a polygon's triangulation has about as many vertices as triangles): about a third of the ground's vertex work, the same triangles in the same order. Then meshoptimizer's vertex-cache order within a fill (design point 4) changes no pixel either: a fill's triangles are one colour at one depth.
5. **A new moment's light off the render thread.** When the moment changes (about once a minute), the render thread lights every drawn tile's faces in binary64 for one frame slot in one frame, and all of them again for the other slot in the next: at the Main board 440,980 faces over the two slots, 72 to 76 ms on the Mac's debug build (the longest tile 12 ms), so two long frames in a row. Lit once and copied into the other slot, the cost halves; lit on a worker ahead of the frame that takes the moment, the frames stay even.
   - Done (28 October, landed from its side branch on the user's word, `lightworker`): a tile's two areas of colours (`BuildingTile::colours_at`, `reveals_at`) are read by both frame slots once lit, so a tile is lit once a moment, into the area the other slot's frame (which may be in flight) does not read; a new moment is lit ahead on the buildings pass's own thread (`passes/Light.cpp`) while the frames still show the one before, and the frame builder shows it once every pass is ready for it (`Pass::ready_for`): no frame lights a view on the render thread. Each area also remembers the revision of the building FillIn asks about that it was lit with, so a tile that holds it lights again when it changes, its walls in the flat colour over the colours lit now or copied from the light ahead.
6. **The window jobs by tile.** Finding the facade walls within reach costs the render thread 0.5 ms at the home on the Mac, with up to 65,000 walls looked at. The ranks' loop, which decides every item's detail bits, can mark the tiles with an item that has a window or facade bit this frame, and the other tiles' runs are skipped whole: the far tiles of every view.

**The first picture and the tiles**

7. **Stage T kept on disk.** A tile's stage T runs each time the tile becomes resident, and on every start: CI's emulator built one dense canal ring tile in 9.8 s (21 October) and completes its first frame after 10 to 17 s, and the Mac's debug build takes 65 ms a tile on average for the home's ten. The goal is the first picture within 0.5 s warm.
   - Each tile's heap bytes and each pass's CPU data are written once built, keyed by the tile's chunk checksum, its level, the stage T version and the style version, and read on the next start or when the tile comes back. A test holds a loaded tile to a built one byte for byte.
   - First the `vk tiles` line names each pass's share of stage T, so the kept tiles start with the passes that cost the most.
   - Found while looking: a resident tile keeps the level it was first built at when the camera crosses a band, since the residency keys a tile by its pack and place. So zooming builds no tiles again today; once step 5's levels build each band's tiles, the kept stage T serves those crossings too.
8. **The workers at normal priority while a visible tile is missing.** The residency's workers run at nice 10 from the first frame, behind every app thread while the app starts. While a visible tile is missing they would run at normal priority, with an ADPF session of their own (the render thread has one), and at background priority again for the prefetch, the windows and the sun.
   - Done (28 October, landed from its side branch on the user's word, `tilebuild`): `scene::WorkerHints` hears when the frame waits on the workers (a tile in view queued or under way) and each piece of the frame's work; on Android each world's `platform::WorkerSession` runs its workers (named atlas-tiles) at nice 0 while the frame waits and nice 10 otherwise, with an ADPF session of their own (16.67 ms the target) told each piece's time; the sun's threads take the same rule.
   - Done (30 October): the packs' own stage T (`scene::PackStage`, the canal names of each pack drawn) runs at nice 10 too: made on the render thread, its thread had kept the display priority.
9. **A dense tile across the workers.** A tile's passes build one after another on one worker, so the picture waits for its longest tile. Built in parallel (their items added in the passes' order after), or the buildings by blocks of cells, the wait is shared.
   - Done (28 October, landed from its side branch on the user's word, `tilebuild`): a worker decodes its tile into a pooled scratch and builds its passes the longest first; a worker with nothing of its own takes a piece of a pass's shared work (`TileBuild::share`, `helpers`), then the queue's next tile, then another tile's pass; the worker finishing a tile's last pass adds the items in the passes' order. The buildings walk a dense tile's records in blocks of about 192, at most 8, joined in order as one walk makes them (`geometry::BuildingInput::share`, `blocks`; the tile's outlines made once with the walk's records).
10. **The sun's bits kept** per tile and sun bucket, as stage T is, so a warm start is lit at once.

**Around the frame**

11. **Even pacing at 60.** The one problem measured on the user's phone: the stand-in's surface gestures dropped 145 to 182 frames each there with the GPU in the low teens of milliseconds (Mali: 99 of 1,813 on a pan). The session says its frames come at 60 a second, and at 120 while the map moves (`ANativeWindow_setFrameRate`, fixed-source compatibility since 3 November, below), and the render loop draws on Choreographer's vsync. The Pixel 10s' displays run at up to 120 Hz: a loop drawing at every vsync there draws twice the frames, and one drawing at 60 into a 120 Hz FIFO can land a frame on one refresh or two. The user's phone has `VK_EXT_present_timing`, `VK_KHR_present_wait2` and FIFO latest-ready (Android 17), and 96 % of the survey's devices `VK_GOOGLE_display_timing`: each frame's actual presentation time, to see where the drops come from, and a target time on a 60 Hz cadence, to pace the frames evenly. VK facts' refresh line says what rate the display runs at.
   - The limit of 60 frames a second (D5.8; a frame at most every second vsync above 100 Hz, which gain 15 left standing) read the display's period wrong until 3 November. It took the mean of the gaps between the vsyncs the loop received, and after a frame longer than a refresh the next one comes two periods on, so at 120 Hz the mean settled at 11 to 12 ms. With the headroom over 0.6 (a warm phone) that read as 90 Hz, and the frames were kept 22 ms apart, past the vsync at 16.7 ms: frames of 7 to 11 ms showed at 40 to 52 a second instead of 60 (the stutter review's simulation of 3 November; alphas 16 to 27).
   - Now `frame::Cadence` (`frame/Cadence.hpp`, asked by `jni/Bridge.cpp` at each vsync) takes the period from the vsync's frame timelines, which SurfaceFlinger spaces by the app's vsync period (API 33); before API 33, from the shortest of the last 16 gaps, each a whole number of periods. The rules stay: every vsync at 60 Hz, every second one at 120, at 90 every vsync where the phone is cool, and no frame that comes later than that held back. The headroom is read at most once a second (`frame::Warmth`), since each read is a call into the system's thermal service (the old estimate made one at every vsync); a reading that is not a number keeps the last that was, where it read as cool, and a phone never read counts as cool.
   - At 90 Hz where the phone runs warm, D5.8 gave every second vsync, 45 frames a second, until 3 November; since then the frames go by the phase of 60 a second (`Cadence::Step::phase`), one vsync and two in turn: each frame is due a sixtieth of a second after the last one's phase, and one that comes a vsync or more after the phase allowed moves the phase on from itself, so a later frame is never held, nor followed by a run of vsyncs to catch up. So neither the vote of 120 nor the touch boost under the finger, which both raise a 60 and 90 Hz display to 90 Hz, costs a warm phone its frames under a period (11.1 ms). A frame over a period misses the next vsync at any step, so at 90 Hz frames of 11.1 to 16.7 ms come 45 a second where 60 Hz gave 60, cool or warm: that is 90 Hz's own cost on such a display, under the finger as under the vote. Gain 15's pacer takes a period for the phase's cadence, since a frame may follow the last at the next vsync.
   - Past about 16 ms of GPU a step of one vsync runs the GPU behind its frames (the review's fourth check of 3 November, its model of FIFO with three images and two frames in flight): the slot's fence and the acquire hold the render thread 1 to 5 ms a frame, and each frame shows 8 to 17 ms later, by 4 to 5 ms more or less than the last, an uneven speed, for 55 or 48 frames a second where every second vsync gives 45 at an even lag. So at 90 Hz the loop draws every second vsync from a GPU figure (an earlier frame's, two frames old, read back after its fence) of 16 ms or more (`Cadence::heavy_gpu_ns`), cool or warm, and by the phase or every vsync again from one under 14 ms (`Cadence::light_gpu_ns`). Between the two the step stays as it was, and ADPF's target with it (`Cadence::gpu_took`; the engine agent's review of 4 November): with the one threshold, a GPU about 16 ms switched the step from frame to frame, and a cool phone's target between 11.1 and 16.7 ms with it. Where the GPU has no timestamps there is no figure, and nothing changes.
   - Held by `cadence_test`: the loop at 60, 90 and 120 Hz with the review's frames of 7 to 11 ms (60 a second, the period the display's at every vsync, the old mean reading the same vsyncs as 90 Hz), 20 ms frames (three refreshes apart at 120 Hz, two at 60), and without timelines; at 120 Hz, cool and warm, frames of about 2 to 22 ms with the review's spread (the next two periods on after one under two periods, at the first vsync after it ends after a longer one; with the old mean it fails both ways, warm frames held to 25 ms and cool ones 8.3 ms apart); at 90 Hz where the phone runs warm, light frames two to every three vsyncs (60 a second), and frames of about 2 to 22 ms with the spread one vsync or two apart under a period, at the first vsync after their end over one, never two within fewer than three vsyncs (the old every second vsync fails it, and so does a phase that never moves on from a later frame); at 90 Hz behind a GPU of 16 ms or more, cool or warm, every second vsync from the first frame that sees the figure, just under it as without one, and a GPU heavy and light in turn followed two frames late each way (the guard left out fails it, and so does a guard where the phone is warm alone); a GPU of 15.5 and 16.5 ms in turn, and one drifting from 13 to 17 ms and back, smooth and with a wobble of 0.9 ms either way from frame to frame, changing the step once a crossing of the band and a cool phone's ADPF target as often, a warm phone's never (the one threshold fails it); and the headroom's reads (once a second, the last number kept). Not checked on the phone.
   - The render thread's ADPF hint (D17) heard the waits as work until 3 November. It reported the frame's whole time, from before stage F to the present's return, with the waits for the slot's previous frame (`gpu/Frames.cpp`: the GPU's) and for the swapchain's image (the display's) in it. A frame the GPU held told ADPF the CPU had missed its 16.7 ms, and the CPU's clocks rose for nothing, on a phone whose CPU and GPU share one thermal budget. It now reports the frame's time less those waits (`FrameReport::wait_ms`, `Drawn::wait_ns`), as Android's own render thread leaves out its dequeue (HWUI's `CanvasContext`); the GPU's figure is as before, an earlier frame's. Gain 15's pacer keeps the waits: they hold a frame's image back as its work does. Held by the swapchain and session tests (the waits within the frame's time). Not checked on the phone.
   - The hint's target was 16.7 ms throughout until 3 November, so at 90 Hz where the phone is cool, with a frame due every 11.1 ms, ADPF heard frames of 12 to 16 ms as on time. It now follows the frames' interval (`Cadence::target_ns`, told with `APerformanceHint_updateTargetWorkDuration` only when it changes, `HintSession::target`): 11.1 ms at 90 Hz where every vsync draws (the phone cool, the GPU light by the band above), 16.7 ms elsewhere (a period at 60 Hz, two at 120, and at 90 Hz a sixtieth of a second by the phase and behind a heavy GPU alike). A warm phone is never asked to fit a step of one vsync: that adds the heat the warm rule exists to avoid (the review's fourth check). Held by `cadence_test` (the target at each step). Not checked on the phone.
   - The vote of 60 had default compatibility until 3 November, and SurfaceFlinger takes a layer that votes so for one that knows the rate it wants: while one is on the display it gives no touch boost (`RefreshRateSelector`: "games that use `setFrameRate` with Default compatibility to limit the frame rate"), so under the finger the Pixel 10s' displays stayed at 60 Hz, where a frame over 16.7 ms shows a whole refresh later. The user's alpha with the Frame timings off showed that: more frames than with them on, but more artefacts and an occasional jitter between them.
   - Now both votes have fixed-source compatibility (ExplicitExactOrMultiple), which leaves the boost alone, and the vote follows the move: 120 from the map's second new camera since the frame loop last rested, 60 once the loop has rested a second with the map still (`frame::Motion`, told by `jni/Bridge.cpp`, to `Session::set_moving`). The touch that starts a move raises the display at once, the vote of 120 holds it through a fling's tail and the camera's animations, and at rest the display goes back to 60 (of 60 and 120, both multiples of the vote, SurfaceFlinger takes the lower) and lower with its idle timer. One camera alone, or a tile or a fade at rest, moves nothing, so the display does not switch for a frame. A vote is said once the frame is out, so that no frame waits for it, and takes effect at once (`BLASTBufferQueue::setFrameRate` applies a transaction, buffers or none); a window attached while the map moves is told 120.
   - The second's hold keeps pan, lift and pan again from switching the display at each rest: SurfaceFlinger's `RefreshRateSelector` switches at once, with no hold of its own, each switch starts its `VSyncPredictor` again, and on some OLED panels a switch shows as a brief step in brightness (the review's checks of 3 November). The render thread's timer (a timerfd on its looper, `RenderLoop::set_timer`) says 60 when the second is up, and the map moving again within it calls the drop off; one camera within it (a location fix) holds nothing longer, so fixes a second apart keep no 120 at rest. Where the thread has no timer, 60 comes at the rest.
   - At 120 Hz the limit above draws every second vsync and each frame shows at the first refresh after it is ready: a frame of 17 to 25 ms shows 25 ms after its vsync, 40 a second, where 60 Hz gave 30. On a 60 and 90 Hz display the vote of 120 lands on 90 where the map's layer is the only one drawing (it scores 90 barely over 60), else on 60, and the limit's rule there is every vsync where the phone is cool (90 a second), the phase where it is warm (60, above), and every second vsync behind a heavy GPU (45). The surface's log line says `frame-rate 60 fixed-source`. Held by `session_vk_test` (60 when attached, 120 and 60 once a change, a window come back while the map moves told 120, one that cannot be told asked once) and `motion_test` (120 from the second new camera, 60 a second after the rest, a move within the second calling the drop off, one camera within it holding nothing longer). Not checked on the phone: the user's phone showing 60 Hz while the map moves with the Frame timings off (Developer options, Show refresh rate) is what says the old vote held it there.
   - The Map look's Fast refresh (3 November; the user: "Give it a toggle?", and the review's fourth check asked for a way to turn the vote off, so that a rate switch's step in brightness can be told from the driver's flicker) is on by default: the vote above. Off, the window is told 60 with default compatibility whatever the map does, the vote of alphas up to 27: no vote of 120, so no hold, and no touch boost while the map is on the display. A change tells the window again at once (`Session::set_fast_refresh`, `Native.setFastRefresh`; the app keeps it as `SettingsKeys.MAP_FAST_REFRESH`, which `MapEngine` hands the core as it starts and as it changes), and the swapchain is untouched; the cadence above, the 90 Hz phase and the GPU's guard included, is the same either way. Its chip, last in the Engine parts' last row, shows only where the display has a mode above 60 Hz on Android 11 or later (`MapEngine.fastRefreshSwitch`): elsewhere neither vote does anything. The surface's log line says `frame-rate 60 default` with it off. Held by `session_vk_test` (60 default when attached with it off, nothing more told as the map moves and rests, 120 and 60 fixed-source once it is on, 60 default again at once, the output never made again, a window come back while the map moves told 60 default), `PartsSettingsTest` (on when unset) and `MineAccessibilityTest` (the chip on a display with a 120 Hz mode, named as the rest, and none on one of 60 Hz alone). Not checked on the phone.
12. **The overlay only when it draws.** The map's Compose canvas redraws at every camera change (`frameTick`), also where it draws nothing (the core's labels, no route, marker, location or tape), so HWUI renders the window over the map every frame, on the same GPU as the core. It need redraw only when it has something to draw. App side; `dumpsys gfxinfo` on the phone shows it.
   - Done (27 October), now that the core draws the markers and the puck: the canvas's draw reads `canvasTick`, its own state's tick, and decides first whether it draws anything over the core's picture (`CanvasWork.idle`: the core's path wanted and none of today's map over it, nothing on the ground, the labels, markers and puck the core's, no tape, tilt readout, debug panel or honest state). Where it draws nothing it hands the core what changed (labels, markers) and reads neither the frames' tick nor the camera, so moving the map draws no window over it. Whatever decides it either is snapshot state (the overlays, the tape, the tilt readout from a tilt's start to the end of its fade, the debug switch) or bumps `canvasTick` when it changes (the core, the path wanted, the transition's cover, where the labels, markers and puck are drawn, the honest state). A tap lays the markers out where the camera is then, since the canvas no longer does at each frame.
   - Held by `CanvasWorkTest`. Since the core draws the routes and areas too, it holds on the route screens as well. Not measured yet: the phone's `dumpsys gfxinfo` over a pan at the home should show no HWUI frames while the map moves.
13. **The CPU code's build.** Profile-guided optimisation of the core's C++ (stages P, T and F) changes no result under its flags (`-ffp-contract=off`, no fast maths) and usually gains a tenth to a fifth. A window's build loads its scene six times (twice at tolerance 0 for the memory budget, and once at each of the four other tolerances for the variants); loading only what a tolerance can change would save most of that, if the parity tests hold it.
14. **The step 2 refinements, after these:** the roofs' strokes' fragments (a frame of quads at the Main board), the soft shadows' boxes in one dispatch, the ground after what stands up (on Mali and Adreno; PowerVR's hidden surface removal already shades an opaque pixel once) and 32-byte wall records (memory and bandwidth).

**Found in the code (25 October)**

Read from the core on 25 October, beside the gains above. 15 and 16 are the likeliest causes of dropped frames while the map moves, 17 to 20 cut GPU and start-up time, and 21 and 22 measure them all. None changes a pixel; each names what holds it.

15. **Pacing by the frame's own time.** Each present asks for the expected presentation of Choreographer's preferred frame timeline (`on_vsync33` in `platform/android/Looper.cpp`, `on_vsync` in `jni/Bridge.cpp`, `desiredPresentTime` in `gpu/Swapchain.cpp`). That timeline is the platform's default schedule for the display, not this frame's work: a frame whose stage F, recording and GPU work run past its deadline is shown a refresh late and the next one on time, a long gap and then a short one. That fits what the stand-in did on the user's phone, frames dropped with the GPU in the low teens of milliseconds.
    - Read every timeline (`AChoreographerFrameCallbackData_getFrameTimelinesLength`, API 33) and take the first whose deadline leaves the frame its recent CPU and GPU time (their p95 over the last frames, which `FrameRecord` already holds).
    - Keep an even cadence: each desired presentation one 60 Hz step after the last, on the display's refresh grid, corrected by the actual presentation times `getPastPresentationTimingGOOGLE` returns (VK bench already reads them); `VK_EXT_present_timing` where the device has it (Android 17, the user's phone).
    - Swappy (`androidx.games:games-frame-pacing`, Apache 2.0, in Unity and Unreal) does this and plugs in at `Swapchain::present`; its 2.1.2 release fixed dropped frames on 120 Hz displays.
    - It costs a refresh of latency (8.3 ms at 120 Hz) and keeps every frame on time: gain 11's means, held by VK bench's presented intervals.
    - Done (26 October), the choice: `frame::Pacer` (`frame/Pacing.hpp`) takes, from the preferred timeline on, the first whose deadline leaves the frame the 90th percentile of its last 32 frames' CPU and GPU time plus 1 ms, and whose presentation is at least the cadence after the last one asked for, less half a period (two periods at 120 Hz, where a frame comes every second vsync); else the last. `jni/Bridge.cpp` reads every timeline Choreographer offers (`getFrameTimelinesLength`) and asks the swapchain for the chosen one's presentation. Before 8 frames are measured, and where the GPU's clock measures nothing, the preferred one stays. Held by `pacing_test`; the phone's presented intervals in VK bench prove it. Still open: the correction by the display's actual presentation times, and `VK_EXT_present_timing`.
    - Gone from alpha 16 (5f9e151; the user, 27 October: "There should be no overhead from smoothing. When the frames are already low more frames despite more stutter is better"): each frame shows as soon as it is ready. From alpha 20 the pacer is back behind a debug build's switch (Engine parts: Frame pacing, off by default; `Core::set_pacing`, `MapEngine.pacing`), because the stop at z 17 on the user's PowerVR came with alpha 16 (the user, 29 October: "I tried alpha 15 again there zooming works with alpha 16 it doesnt anymore"): on the phone, the switch tells whether its going is the cause.
    - Undone (27 October), the user: "There should be no overhead from smoothing. When the frames are already low more frames despite more stutter is better", and "it makes slow phones feel less responsive". A later timeline puts a refresh or more between a touch and its frame, and holds the frame's image in the queue for that long, so on a phone that can't keep up the pacing cost both latency and frames. Every frame now asks for no presentation (as soon as it is ready, the first refresh it makes), and the frame loop reads only Choreographer's frame time and preferred timeline; `frame::Pacer` and `pacing_test` are gone. A slow phone shows uneven gaps instead of late frames. Gain 11's limit on displays faster than 100 Hz (a frame at most every second refresh) stays: it never holds back a frame that comes later than that, so it costs a slow phone nothing (once it reads the display's period right: until 3 November it did not, gain 11).
16. **The render thread never waits for a worker.** Every frame the heaps' provider of set 1 (`HeapGlobals::update` in `scene/World.cpp`) asks `Heaps::buffer()`, which takes the heaps' shared lock. A worker growing the heaps holds that lock exclusively while it makes the larger buffer, copies every byte of the old one and flushes it (`Heaps::grow`): from 8 MB to 16, 32 and 64, the last copying 32 MB. A tile that lands while the map moves can grow them, and that frame waits through the copy.
    - The buffer's handle and size in an atomic the render thread reads without the lock: the old buffer already lives until the frames that bound it finish (`old_`).
    - Better, no copy at all: the heaps grow by a block of their own (`HeapRange` already carries a block number) instead of doubling one buffer.
    - Then every other lock the render thread takes each frame, each under a trace section (21), so none waits on a worker for long.
    - Done (26 October), all but the blocks: a frame takes the buffer it binds with the marking of what a growth replaced (`Heaps::bind`), under the render lock alone, which a growth takes only to swap the buffers after its copy; the stats read the capacity from an atomic and the ranges under the blocks' lock, which a growth no longer holds through its copy. So no frame waits for a growth. It also closes a race: a growth between a frame's reading of the buffer and its marking could have let go of the buffer that frame bound once the frame before it finished. Held by the heaps' tests (`scene_vk_test`).
17. **The soft shadows' buffers per frame slot.** Their offscreen work opens with a barrier on every earlier vertex, fragment and compute stage (`record_shadow_mask` in `passes/Shadows.cpp`: the box anew once the last frame has read the grid), because one grid, one mask and one chain of blur buffers serve every frame. So each frame's shadows wait for all of the last frame's fragment shading: on a tiler the next frame's compute and binning cannot overlap the last frame's shading, and the GPU's time is their sum instead of the larger. With a set per frame slot, as the buildings' kept lists have (`passes/Kept.cpp`), the barriers order only the frame's own work, for a second copy of the mask and the blur's buffers (R8, the frame's size). Any other pass whose early work rewrites what the last frame's late work read goes the same way. Held by `shadows_vk_test` and the engine's references.
    - Done (28 October, landed from its side branch on the user's word, `shadowslots`): each frame slot has its own mask, grid, blur buffers, framebuffer, blur sets and set 3 (`SoftShadowSlot`, `Ground::Pipelines::soft_sets`), which only the frame before it in the slot read, so a frame's shadows no longer wait for every earlier frame's work, nor its blur for the last frame's composite; the CPU writes the box's start into the slot's mapped grid, and the ground's offscreen phases go after every pass's decide phase (the shadows' mask, the ripple's layer, the lit streets' layers). The barriers go from 10 a frame to 7; the second set costs 9.1 MB at S1. The ripple's and the lit streets' buffers are shared across the slots' sets, each behind barriers on the last frame's composite. Held by `shadows_vk_test`, `slots_vk_test` (twelve frames back to back, the validation layer's synchronization check silent).
18. **Coverage from the fragment stage only where it must be.** The trees, the items' faces and parts, the windows, and the ground's lines, quays, glints and rings decide their samples in the fragment stage, write `SV_Coverage` and discard where no sample is covered (`shaders/tree/draw.slang`, `shaders/item/draw.slang`, the windows in `shaders/building/draw.slang`, `shaders/ground/ground.slang`). A pipeline whose fragment stage can discard or write its mask loses the early depth path: Mali's forward pixel kill and early depth writes, and PowerVR's hidden-surface removal before shading (such draws are its punch-through, and the opaque draws after them wait). The shapes stay exact, and the fast path comes back where it can:
    - a variant without the mask for primitives the rasterizer covers as the shader would: an item's plain face (`coverage_of` gives every sample when `kind.y` is 0) runs in the discarding pipeline today;
    - the discarding draws after the plain opaque ones, where true depth leaves their order free;
    - proxies that hug their shapes (a hull, not a box), so fewer fragments run only to be discarded; the overdraw twin counts them before and after.
    - Held by the twin, L1 and each pass's raster against the twin.
    - Done (26 October), the items' plain fills: at true depth `Items::record_draw` draws the faces' fans first, alone, through `items_fills_fs`, which writes no mask and discards nothing, so a tiler keeps its early depth test and hidden-surface removal for them, then the edges alone through `items_faces_fs` (`items_faces_vs` places a face's fan or its edges' quads as the push's y asks). A box's faces lead one another (items.slangh), so no edge of an earlier face ties a later face's fan in depth, and the two draws' order decides no sample; under the painter switch, where an element's parts share its slot's depth, the faces draw in one draw as before. Held by `items_vk_test` (every configuration, at rest and moving, at true depth and under the painter switch: every pixel the exact raster's as before) and the engine's references (8 of 8 as version 24).
    - Done (28 October, landed from its side branch on the user's word, `plainfills`): the items' faces split from their edges only at true depth with float depth (on a 24-bit depth buffer a lead falls under the depth's step beyond about 77 m, so a box's faces draw in one draw there as before); the trees' and the street furniture's quads below depth 1 through `trees_fills_vs`/`trees_fills_fs`, which write no mask and discard nothing, so a tiler keeps its early depth test for them: the close trees' trunks after the rest, the lamps' caps and lanterns and the boxes' tops before it. Held by `items_vk_test`, `trees_vk_test` and the engine's references.
19. **The drizzle's rings over the water's rows.** `record_rings` draws one triangle over the whole frame (`passes/Rings.cpp`), so `rings_fs` runs at every pixel and sample, and outside the bands it only searches them and finds none. Drawn over the bands' rows alone (a rect each), it runs only where a ring can land. Inside, the bisection of up to 64 steps (`ellipse_distance`) need only run where the ellipse's own measure cannot decide: g = sqrt((x/a)² + (y/b)²) bounds the distance to the ring between b·|g − 1| and a·|g − 1| (b the smaller semi-axis), which settles the samples clearly inside or outside the stroke, with a margin for float rounding; the bisection runs where the bounds straddle the half width, and the same samples are kept. Held by `rings_vk_test`. The next full-frame effects (the ripple, the eye-level glints) start bounded the same way.
    - Done (26 October), the bisection's half: `rings_fs` takes the ellipse's own bounds first (e.y · |g − 1| ≤ distance ≤ e.x · |g − 1|, a margin of .001 tile px from the half width), so a sample in a ring's box needs the bisection only where the bounds straddle the stroke: 38% of the in-box tests over the tile's pattern, against all of them before. Every sample is decided as the bisection alone decides it: `rings_vk_test`'s 2,530 pixels under the rings are unchanged (2 at a stroke's edge, as before), and so are the engine's wet references. The bands' rects stay undone: at every map pitch the one band is the whole frame, and at eye level the water's stencil already keeps the fragments to the water's rows.
20. **Pipelines made across the cores.** `FrameBuilder::prepare` makes every pass's pipelines one after another before the first frame. On the user's phone an empty cache took 285 ms for the stand-in's 10 (18 October); the map's passes make theirs at 43 call sites, and every alpha's new shaders miss the cache at its first launch. Made on several threads at once (pipeline creation and the pipeline cache are thread-safe), those the first picture needs first, the first picture after an update comes sooner; the crash guard then names every pipeline under way, not one. The cache is saved as now.
    - Done (26 October): `FrameBuilder::prepare` runs every pass's prepare on a thread of its own, the calling one among them, one a core (`FrameOptions::threads`), so an empty cache costs the longest pass instead of the sum of them. A pass's prepare touches only its own state and its context, and what the passes share takes several threads at once: the device and its allocator, the pipeline cache (its mark of a pipeline made is atomic now), the frame's layout and the log. Every pass's pipelines are the first picture's, so they all start at once where there are cores for them, in the registry's order where there are not. The crash guard's marker names every module and pipeline under way, a line each, and the next start's `vk guard` line names them all, separated by "; "; with one under way both read as before. Through an empty cache on lavapipe (the Mac's debug build, the registry's 64 pipelines, the Mac shared with other work throughout), prepare took 5.4 to 6.1 s before and takes 1.9 to 2.2 s on eight threads (4.5 to 5.4 s on one, in the same runs), and 1.4 to 1.5 s in the engine references' run, a build without sanitizers. The longest passes bound it now, the buildings' (about 1.8 s, the windows' pipeline 1.0 s of it) and the items' (about 1.6 s, their compute pipeline 1.5 s alone), so a pass's own pipelines made across the threads would bring it to about 1.5 s here, no lower.
